        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

  "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."
}

> DEVELOPER

Execute the approved plan in the repository.

        Project directory:
        /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan

        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

        Batch framing:
        - Execute batch 3 of 8.
        - Actionable task IDs for this batch: ['T3']
        - Already completed task IDs available as dependency context: ['T1', 'T2']

        Actionable tasks for this batch:
        [
  {
    "id": "T3",
    "description": "Implement _take_observation helper in megaplan/loop/engine.py. Takes output buffer, elapsed seconds, and spec. Extracts tail of output (last 4000 chars).   "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."

> DEVELOPER

Execute the approved plan in the repository.

        Project directory:
        /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan

        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

(2) Break pattern \u2014 process prints 'FATAL ERROR' after delay, verify early termination and action='break_pattern'.   "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."
}

> DEVELOPER

Execute the approved plan in the repository.

        Project directory:
        /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan

        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

  "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."
}

> DEVELOPER

Execute the approved plan in the repository.

        Project directory:
        /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan

        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

  "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."
}

        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

Must return same dict shape as _run_user_command (returncode, stdout, stderr, timed_out, output via existing truncation) PLUS observations list. On terminate, use process.terminate() then process.wait(5), falling back to process.kill() if wait times out (FLAG-001). Join reader threads after process ends.   "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."
}

> DEVELOPER

Execute the approved plan in the repository.

        Project directory:
        /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan

        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

Must return same dict shape as _run_user_command (returncode, stdout, stderr, timed_out, output via existing truncation) PLUS observations list. On terminate, use process.terminate() then process.wait(5), falling back to process.kill() if wait times out (FLAG-001). Join reader threads after process ends.   "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."
}

> DEVELOPER

Execute the approved plan in the repository.

        Project directory:
        /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan

        Idea:
Design and implement a process monitoring/observation mechanism for MegaLoop.

## Problem

MegaLoop currently runs a command, waits for it to complete, then analyzes the output. But many real workloads are long-running (training runs, eval suites, deploy pipelines) where you want to CHECK IN periodically — read logs, check metrics, decide whether to continue or kill early.

The agent needs a way to: (1) launch a process, (2) periodically observe its output/logs/artifacts while it runs, (3) decide based on observations whether to let it continue, kill it early, or intervene.

## Design questions to resolve

1. **Wait/sleep mechanism**: Should the loop use a sleep-then-check pattern? A tool that blocks for N seconds then returns control? A polling loop? What's the right primitive for an agentic system that needs to observe a process over time?

2. **Observation strategy**: How does the agent read partial output? Options: tail the log file, read the last N lines of stdout, check for specific artifact files, poll a metrics endpoint. What's the right abstraction?

3. **Break conditions**: How does the agent decide to break? LLM-judged (read the output, decide if it looks stuck/diverging/done)? Metric-based (extract a number, compare to threshold)? Both?

4. **Skill integration**: The megaloop skill doc drives the orchestration. How should the observation/monitoring phase integrate with the existing plan→execute cycle? Is it a sub-phase of execute? A separate phase? A tool the execute sub-agent uses?

5. **Implementation**: What's the minimal code to add to megaplan/loop/ to support this? A new tool? A modification to run_execute_phase? A wrapper around subprocess that yields control periodically?

## What to learn from

- The existing megaplan/loop/engine.py run_execute_phase which currently runs subprocess to completion
- The /loop skill doc pattern for how the agent drives the loop
- autoresearch's pattern of fixed time budgets (5 min) — they just wait for completion but cap the time
- Common patterns in agentic systems for process monitoring (poll loops, callback hooks, signal files)

## Constraints

- Must work within Claude Code's tool execution model (the agent calls tools, tools return results)
- Should be minimal — extend the existing loop infrastructure, don't rebuild it
- The agent should be able to check in at configurable intervals
- Must handle both fast processes (seconds) and slow ones (hours)

        "python - <<'PY'\nimport subprocess\nimport tempfile\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nfrom megaplan.cli import build_parser\nfrom megaplan.loop.engine import load_loop_state\nfrom megaplan.loop.handlers import handle_loop_init\n\nwith tempfile.TemporaryDirectory() as tmp:\n    root = Path(tmp) / 'root'\n    project_dir = Path(tmp) / 'project'\n    root.mkdir()\n    project_dir.mkdir()\n    (project_dir / 'train.py').write_text(\"print('train')\\n\", encoding='utf-8')\n    subprocess.run(['git', 'init'], cwd=project_dir, check=True, capture_output=True, text=True)\n    subprocess.run(['git', 'config', 'user.email', 'test@example.com'], cwd=project_dir, check=True)\n    subprocess.run(['git', 'config', 'user.name', 'Test User'], cwd=project_dir, check=True)\n    subprocess.run(['git', 'add', 'train.py'], cwd=project_dir, check=True)\n    subprocess.run(['git', 'commit', '-m', 'initial'], cwd=project_dir, check=True, capture_output=True, text=True)\n\n    parser = build_parser()\n    args = parser.parse_args([\n        'loop-init',\n        '--project-dir', str(project_dir),\n        '--command', 'python train.py',\n        '--name', 'demo-loop',\n        '--goal', 'train model',\n        '--observe-interval', '30',\n        '--observe-break-patterns', 'CUDA OOM,NaN loss',\n    ])\n\n    import os\n    os.environ['MEGAPLAN_MOCK_WORKERS'] = '1'\n    response = handle_loop_init(root, args)\n    state = load_loop_state(project_dir, 'demo-loop')\n    print('response_step', response['step'])\n    print('saved_interval', state['spec'].get('observe_interval'))\n    print('saved_break_patterns', state['spec'].get('observe_break_patterns'))\n    print('status_monitoring_active', response['details']['monitoring']['active'])\nPY",

  "watch_items": [
    "FLAG-003 (significant): Early-killed baselines pass truncated output to execute worker. When implementing T5/T6, the prompt MUST explicitly tell the worker that output is truncated and include the kill reason \u2014 otherwise the worker will misdiagnose partial output as a different failure mode.",
    "FLAG-001: process.terminate() without kill() fallback can leave zombies. In T2, after terminate(), call process.wait(timeout=5) and fall back to process.kill() if TimeoutError.",
    "FLAG-002: Metric stall detection must know which direction is 'improvement'. In T3, check spec for optimization_strategy or use _is_better which already handles direction. Do not hardcode 'lower is better'.",
    "FLAG-004: observe_metric_stall may duplicate outer-loop metric comparison logic. Keep the inner observation stall check simple (N consecutive flat readings) \u2014 don't replicate the full _is_better/_is_worse comparison that the outer loop already does.",
    "FLAG-006: _run_monitored_command must return the same dict shape as _run_user_command including the 'output' key formatted by _command_output (truncated combined stdout+stderr). Don't break callers that expect this key.",
    "FLAG-005: Plan references loop/schemas.py which doesn't exist. Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is deferred, so no schema file needed for v1. If a schema is needed later, register it in the existing megaplan/schemas.py.",
    "DEBT: worker-permissions \u2014 codex --full-auto and claude --permission-mode flags only trigger for step=='execute', not 'loop_execute'. Do not make this worse. If adding new worker calls (e.g., observe worker), ensure they use the existing loop_execute permission path.",
    "Output buffer memory: Cap at 200K chars rolling window. For multi-hour training runs, unbounded buffering could exhaust memory.",
    "Thread safety: Reader threads append to a shared list. Use a lock if reading the buffer from the main thread while readers are still running, or accept that list.append is thread-safe in CPython (GIL).",
    "Backward compatibility: Any loop without observe_interval set must behave identically to current code. This is the single most important invariant."
  ],

  "meta_commentary": "Execution guidance:\n\n**Sequencing matters.** T1\u2192T2\u2192T3 is the core foundation and must be done strictly in order. T4 (unit tests) should run immediately after T3 to validate the subprocess monitoring before wiring it into the execute phase. This catches issues early \u2014 threading bugs and timing problems are much easier to debug in isolation than when layered into the full execute flow.\n\n**The critical risk is FLAG-003.** When a monitored baseline gets killed early (break pattern match, metric stall), the execute worker receives truncated output. If the prompt doesn't explicitly flag this, the worker will try to diagnose a \"failure\" that's actually just incomplete output. T6 handles this \u2014 make the prompt loud about truncation. The template should say something like \"Process was terminated early (break pattern: 'CUDA OOM' matched) at 45s. The output below is INCOMPLETE.\"\n\n**Threading is standard but easy to get wrong.** The reader thread pattern (T2) is textbook Python \u2014 daemon threads reading from Popen pipes into a list. CPython's GIL makes list.append safe, but if you need to read the buffer mid-flight (for observations), slice the list rather than iterating it. Join both threads after the process exits to ensure cleanup.\n\n**Timing tests will be flaky.** T4 tests involve real sleep() calls and process launching. Use generous tolerances (\u00b150% on timing assertions). Consider using a fast observe_interval (1-2s) and short-lived test processes to keep the test suite fast. If a test checks \"observations collected at roughly correct intervals,\" assert count within a range, not exact equality.\n\n**Don't over-engineer observe_metric_stall.** FLAG-004 correctly notes this partially duplicates outer-loop logic. Keep it simple: if the last N extracted metrics are identical (within epsilon), declare stall. Don't try to detect regression trends \u2014 the outer loop's _is_better/_is_worse handles that after the iteration completes.\n\n**Step 3 (LLM-judged observation) is explicitly deferred.** Don't build coupling points for it. The observation data structure (Observation TypedDict) is sufficient \u2014 a future LLM observer can consume the same snapshots without schema changes. Don't add observe_llm_interval to LoopSpec yet.\n\n**Output buffer cap:** 200K chars rolling window. Implement as: when buffer exceeds threshold, trim from the front. The 'output' key in the return dict should use existing _command_output truncation (12000 chars) applied to the final buffer contents \u2014 don't change that contract."
}

        Debt watch items (do not make these worse):
- [DEBT] execute-phase-contract: early-killed baselines pass truncated output to the execute worker, which may produce lower-quality diagnosis than full output. (flagged 1 times across 1 plans)
- [DEBT] worker-permissions: codex --full-auto flag only triggers for step == 'execute', not 'loop_execute' (flagged 1 times across 1 plans)
- [DEBT] worker-permissions: claude --permission-mode bypasspermissions only triggers for step == 'execute', not 'loop_execute' (flagged 1 times across 1 plans)

        - Only produce `sense_check_acknowledgments` for these sense checks: [SC8]
